#316. New Democratic Congress will likely strip the gilding from Trump's Golden Dome

“There’s no doubt that whatever it’s going to be called, it won’t be called Golden Dome.”

Two of the top lawmakers backing the Pentagon’s Golden Dome missile defense shield are among the wave of Republicans leaving Congress in the coming Democratic takeover, raising questions about the future of President Donald Trump’s signature second-term defense initiative.

The shakeup on Capitol Hill comes after military officials signaled in recent months that they might backpedal from one element Trump asked for in Golden Dome: the capability to shoot down missiles in their initial boost phase shortly after launch.

The Golden Dome has escaped serious congressional scrutiny since Trump announced the program in January 2025, one week into his second term in the White House. That lack of scrutiny will change as Democrats take responsibility for writing the Pentagon’s budget, introduce limits on funding, and put forth bills to strip out Golden Dome’s most controversial component and give the program a new name.

That’s the opinion of a half-dozen current and former officials in industry and government. Ars spoke with sources before and after last week’s congressional elections to get a sense of how a new Democratic Congress might make its imprint on missile defense.

It’s important to know that much of what the Trump administration calls “Golden Dome” was already in development (or in some cases, operational) when Trump signed the executive order outlining the initiative 22 months ago. Trump’s order created a new program to consolidate the Pentagon’s existing missile defense efforts, accelerate work already underway on new Ground-Based Interceptors (GBIs), and deploy a new layer of orbiting sensors and Space-Based Interceptors (SBIs) to defend the US homeland against both traditional ballistic missiles and new emerging threats like drones and hypersonic weapons.

The Golden Dome was immediately politicized. The first tranche of its funding—approximately $24 billion—was part of Trump’s so-called “One Big Beautiful Bill,” along with some of the White House’s other contentious policy priorities. The bill passed Congress on a party-line vote. This meant the budget for Golden Dome was not part of the normal military budgeting process in Congress, which usually involves both parties. Instead, it passed Congress through a controversial shortcut known as reconciliation.

Sen. Mike Rounds (R-S.D.) said in a September hearing that the White House’s decision to link Golden Dome’s first tranche of funding to a partisan reconciliation bill was unnecessary. “This should have been on a bipartisan basis,” Rounds said. Indeed, Capitol Hill Republicans refused the Trump administration’s effort to add $350 billion to the Pentagon’s budget this year through a similar reconciliation mechanism.

Lawmakers also formed an all-Republican “Golden Dome Caucus” soon after the unveiling of Golden Dome. Seven House members of the caucus either lost their reelection bids or did not run for reelection at all in the 2026 midterms. These include the caucus’s co-chair, Rep. Jeff Crank (R-Colo.), and the chair of the House subcommittee overseeing Golden Dome, Rep. Scott DesJarlais (R-Tenn.). Both are among the Golden Dome’s biggest cheerleaders in Congress.

The Democratic ranking member of the House Subcommittee on Strategic Forces, Rep. Scott Moulton (D-Mass.), is retiring from Congress. That leaves Rep. John Garamendi (D-Calif.) as the next-senior Democrat on the subcommittee, with a path to becoming chairman when the new Congress convenes in January. In remarks earlier this year, he called Golden Dome a “massive, extraordinarily expensive, and ultimately inadequate defensive system.”

“When you use the words Golden Dome, now it’s political, and when you politicize national security, you get a political response, so it is damaging,” said John Plumb, who supervised military space policy for the Biden administration, in a panel discussion at the CyberSat conference this week.

The Trump administration projects Golden Dome will cost $185 billion over the next 10 years, a figure that’s hard to verify with the scarce information military officials have released about how the system will work.

Other cost estimates are significantly higher. The Congressional Budget Office concluded that a national missile defense system meeting the requirements set out in Trump’s executive order would cost about $1.2 trillion. The American Enterprise Institute released a report last year with a menu of potential Golden Dome architectures, ranging from a quarter-trillion dollars (without SBIs) to $3.6 trillion at the high end.

Pentagon officials chafe at these numbers, claiming they do not take into account the military’s assumptions for low-cost launches and mass-produced satellite networks.

The military envisions at least hundreds, and perhaps thousands, of Space-Based Interceptors in the uppermost layer of Golden Dome. These interceptors would loiter in orbit to wait for a missile attack. SBIs have never been placed into orbit before, despite decades of research and development. The idea has been around at least since the 1980s, when former President Ronald Reagan pushed the Strategic Defense Initiative, or “Star Wars,” weapons program.

“The most ambitious element of Golden Dome is the Space-Based Interceptor layer, and that is going to face significant technical challenges,” said Sen. Mark Kelly (D-Ariz.), in a congressional hearing earlier this year.

“The cost, the physics, the likely reliability, in my view, do not support a full-scale space-based intercept architecture,” said Kelly, a former Navy combat pilot and astronaut. “This means that we could spend more than a trillion dollars and end up with a system that’s not going to work, and it’s not going to meaningfully improve our security.”

Some critics call out Golden Dome’s potential negative impact on global stability, fearing it could spark an arms race driving the world’s other nuclear powers to expand their arsenals. “It motivates the other side to just build more missiles because the easiest way to overcome a defense like that is just to buy more,” said Frank Kendall, who served as secretary of the Air Force in the Biden administration.

Others point to the risks of weaponizing space—a similar argument used against the Reagan-era Star Wars program. Secretary of the Air Force Troy Meink lifted that taboo in September with the first public disclosure that the US Space Force already has weapons in orbit.

But there’s more to the story than SBIs. The satellites and radars that detect and track missile launches are either in use today or were already in development during previous administrations. The same goes for Ground-Based Interceptors, which have been deployed for decades to take out missiles as they approach their targets. Under President Biden, the Pentagon’s Missile Defense Agency awarded Lockheed Martin a $17 billion contract to develop a next-generation Ground-Based Interceptor to defend against more sophisticated missile threats.

The $24 billion Congress approved for Golden Dome last year was a one-time allocation for fiscal year 2026, which ended on October 1. The program’s funding dried up last month because the government is currently operating under a continuing resolution—an extension of last year’s regular military budget, which, of course, did not include the $24 billion Golden Dome infusion routed through reconciliation. The preliminary funding bills in the House and Senate for fiscal year 2027, if passed as-is, would provide only a fraction of the more than $17 billion the Trump administration requested for Golden Dome in this budget cycle.

That’s because the Trump administration again separated a significant part of its record $1.5 trillion topline military budget request into a reconciliation package outside of the normal congressional appropriations process. The supplemental $350 billion funding request—which included funds for Golden Dome—went nowhere in the Republican-controlled Congress. And it won’t happen when Democrats take the gavels next year, either.

It’s impossible to judge the realism of the Pentagon’s aim to get a first draft of Golden Dome up and running by the end 0f 2028, before the end of Trump’s presidency, because so much of the system is classified—but skepticism is the safer bet. Military officials have said little about Golden Dome’s design, architecture, or concept of operations, citing a national security imperative for secrecy.

One thing we do know is that a homeland defense shield like Golden Dome will need international partners to host radar sensors, ground stations, and missile batteries. Canada is an obvious target for such a partnership, and a fully deployed Golden Dome would notionally provide protection over all of North America.

However, this part of the program is off to a slow start. Michael Guetlein, the four-star Space Force general in charge of Golden Dome development, said earlier this year that tariffs—a core Trump administration policy—were limiting his interactions with foreign governments.

So what can we expect when the next Congress convenes with Democratic majorities in January? The consensus among the experts Ars interviewed is that military leaders will still push for new options in missile defense, a need they say is underscored by their experience during the US war with Iran and Russia’s persistent missile strikes on Ukraine.

Modern missiles and drones can easily dodge existing defense systems. The stark expense of existing Western interceptors compared to the cost of Iranian missiles and drones can make defenders think twice about whether to shoot or keep their powder dry for a future attack.

“I feel like the entire effort would benefit from a better problem statement,” one former military space official told Ars. “Why do we think we need something that we don’t currently have? What’s changed? What’s different? What is it?”

This official offered some answers to these questions: “We are not in the 1970s or 1980s anymore, where it was us and the Soviet Union. The vast share of strategic threats to us were strategic ballistic missiles that we could see coming. That’s how things used to be.”

“Today’s environment is different,” he continued. “You have a host of strategic threats that are making classic ballistic missiles shrink in share. You’ve got hypersonic weapons. You’ve got endo-atmospheric hypersonic vehicles. You have increasingly accurate and harder to detect cruise missiles. It’s no longer just Russia. It’s China now, and to a lesser extent, North Korea and Iran.”

These new missile threats and the ubiquity of one-way attack drones make US and allied bases more susceptible to attack, the official said.

“What do we do about it? Do we just build more Mutually Assured Destruction platforms, or is there something else we can do? I think that’s what gets you to maybe we should look at ways to at least interfere with a potential attack against us.”

But some parts of Golden Dome, if followed to the letter of Trump’s executive order, simply may not work, at least at the scale required for global coverage, the official told Ars. One of them is a requirement to develop SBIs that can destroy enemy missiles during their boost phase soon after launch. This is in contrast to midcourse intercept in space or terminal intercept as the missile homes in on its target.

“The boost-phase intercept is a much bigger challenge,” the former military official said. “I’m probably going to have to have an interceptor that can survive reentry, track through reentry, and hit something while it’s still in the atmosphere. Hitting something in midcourse, as it’s left the atmosphere and is in space is just an easier problem… The original executive order talked about boost-phase intercept, and I was always skeptical about that one.”

An SBI aimed at a boost-phase ballistic missile would have to look down toward the Earth, not up toward space. The interceptor would have to distinguish the missile it’s tracking against the background of Earth, with clouds, cities, land, and water. It’s much easier to do that against the cold blackness of space. There are some benefits to a boost-phase intercept. For example, the ballistic missile is traveling slower soon after launch, and you might have another shot to intercept it if you miss.

The window for intercepting a missile in boost phase is also exceptionally narrow. The time from a launch detection to a shoot-down decision might be less than a minute, and a commander charged with making a “kill decision” would likely want more time to understand what’s happening before pulling the trigger on an interceptor.

“When does the person who has the authority for weapons engagement release the weapon? If your answer is it’s all automated, that’s interesting, but it’s also a little scary,” the former military official said. “Now I’m going to explain to the president why you just killed three taikonauts (Chinese astronauts) coming off a launch pad because no human was there. If you do need a human to say, ‘OK, I authorize release of the weapon,’ then that’s a time gap.”

Guetlein, the man in charge of Golden Dome, has acknowledged this challenge.

“I do believe that the technology exists to get after that threat today, and we believe we can solve it, but… can I do it at scale, and can I do it affordably? That’s going to be the huge challenge for boost-phase intercept,” Guetlein told lawmakers in an April hearing. “I will tell you, because we are so focused on affordability, if we cannot do it affordably, we will not go into production.”

There are other ways to take down missiles if engineers can’t close the case for boost-phase intercept, Guetlein said. “If boost-phase intercept from space is not affordable and [doesn’t] scale well, we will not produce it because we have other options to get after it.”

If proven feasible, directed-energy weapons using microwave or laser beams would provide US forces near-endless “magazine depth” in responding to massive salvos of missiles and drones, eschewing the need for “kinetic” SBIs for missile defense. Sen. Angus King (I-Maine) said in a hearing earlier this year that the Space Force should spend more on directed-energy research than on space-based kinetic kill vehicles.

Plumb, the former Biden military space policy chief, pointed to bipartisan support for the Pentagon’s existing missile defense programs, along with new initiatives to improve tracking hypersonic missiles. “I think any administration, Republican or Democrat, is going to continue to improve missile defenses for the US, 100 percent,” he said, adding that it is “probably unlikely” that SBIs survive the coming cull.

So what do the people actually building Golden Dome think? Their answer is similar. Some missile defense priorities will survive, but maybe with a new name.

The name Golden Dome is a “brand,” said Dylan Taylor, chairman and CEO of Voyager Technologies, which works with Anduril on SBIs. “It’s a proxy for layers of a protective onion, if you will, and there are elements of what is being called Golden Dome, where the technology is very advanced, and it works. The systems work. They’re more functional than I think people have reported on or have indicated.”

“Golden Dome is a lot more than SBIs,” said another executive at a space company working on the project. “It’s one layer and element. We believe that there’s bipartisan support for this capability because it is a required capability for national security. The question is whether it can be achieved at a price that makes it executable and palatable. The technical aspect of this closes, and there’s just a question about scalability and cost.”

The executive told Ars that newer, smaller space companies can do the job for less money than the Pentagon’s legacy contractors. Many companies are spending private capital on Golden Dome-related research and development with the promise of future Pentagon business. Military leaders have added stops on Wall Street and with venture capital firms to their usual tours of bases, command centers, and test ranges.

“There’s real hardware being built by all the players,” the official said, suggesting optimism about fielding a working preliminary version of Golden Dome by 2028. “From us, on our execution to date, we’ve conducted engine tests and thermal tests and communications tests.

“My fiduciary responsibility is to turn our investment into these critical technologies into future value, and I wouldn’t have made that call had I not been comfortable with the survivability of the program, assuming we can achieve the technical and financial targets, which we’ve convinced ourselves we can,” the official said.

Rocket Lab is another company with business ties to Golden Dome. The company’s founder and CEO, Peter Beck, told Ars he was convinced missile defense will remain a priority no matter who controls Congress or the White House.

“Is it a national security imperative that we go to the Moon? Probably not. Is it a national security imperative that we can protect the nation from incoming missile threats? Yes,” Beck said. “With these sorts of things, I’d say it’s what shape and scale they are, not whether they survive.”

“There are elements of Golden Dome that I don’t think are going to be politically vulnerable, and there will be other parts of Golden Dome that, of course, will be,” Taylor said. “The point I want to emphasize is people who say, ‘Well, Golden Dome is dead; the whole thing is dead,’ they don’t necessarily understand that there are four or five or six layers to the system, and some are vulnerable and some are not.”

Voyager’s expertise for missile defense is in advanced propulsion, and Taylor said those technologies are relevant for a range of systems, not just Golden Dome.

“There’s no doubt that whatever it’s going to be called, it won’t be called Golden Dome, and there will definitely be machinations in the system, but it won’t be a ‘throw the baby out with the bath water’ approach,” Taylor said. “Given the risk profile, I don’t see that happening.”

In a Senate hearing in September, Tom Karako, director of the Missile Defense Project at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, proposed the idea of a name change as one option to “depoliticize” the missile defense issue.

“It is no secret that the ‘Golden Dome’ moniker has contributed to political polarization around the effort,” Karako said in his testimony. “Several options exist to depolarize and depoliticize the effort. It belongs to Congress to name or rename executive departments—witness the pending proposal to rename the Department of Defense as the Department of War. Congress likewise has legislative power to rename the Golden Dome initiative in a future national defense authorization act.”

“There are things in the program that make sense,” said Sen. Kelly, who serves on the Senate Subcommittee for Strategic Forces. These include new capabilities for “detecting launches,” he said. “The Space-Based Interceptor part is really, really hard, and the physics clearly favors the offense,” Kelly said. “We could go down this road, spend hundreds of billions of dollars, and not get any return on that investment, and missile defense is too important to pursue this just based on aspiration alone.”

Kathy Warden, the CEO of Northrop Grumman, acknowledged last year that with SBIs, “more invention is required than other parts of the [Golden Dome] architecture.”

The most recent trends in warfare, as seen in the Middle East and Ukraine, highlight the need for cheaper, mass-produced countermeasures to use against massive drone and missile attacks. In other words, it requires magazine depth, as some military commanders like to say. Democrats seem likely to conclude that the path to magazine depth does not include SBIs.

#315. What the tiny blue dot on your Android phone is telling you

You're scrolling through your phone when you spot it: a small blue dot sitting quietly near your battery icon. It wasn't there a minute ago, and now it's gone again just as fast. If you've wondered if it's a glitch, don't worry — we all have. It's not a notification you missed or even a problem or a bug. The blue dot is actually one of Android's built-in security features designed to keep you informed about what's happening on your device in real time. More specifically, it tells you when an app is accessing your location data.

Rather than burying this information in a settings menu you'd have to dig through, Android makes it easy to see right alongside all your other notification icons. The blue dot matters more than it might seem because location data is some of the most sensitive information your phone collects. It can reveal where you live, work, buy your groceries and spend your free time. Apps access this data for legitimate reasons, like turn-by-turn directions for your driving companion or local weather. But if you see the dot flashing on and off when you haven't opened a navigation app, you may want to do a bit of sleuthing.

The blue dot is Android's visual indicator that an app is actively tracking your device's location. It typically pops up in the top-right corner of your screen beside other status icons like Wi-Fi, battery and signal bars. When it shows up, an app on your phone is pulling location data through GPS, Wi-Fi signals or nearby cellular towers to figure out where you are.

Basically, the dot isn't meant to track every historical instance of location access, but rather to flag what's happening right now or what just happened moments ago. If you spot the dot while you're using a maps app, that's perfectly expected. If it pops up when you haven't touched an app or website that should need your location, it's time to dig more.

Swiping down from the top of your screen and tapping the indicator typically reveals which app triggered it, giving you an immediate answer without having to guess.

One small mention here — older One UI versions on Samsung phones only displayed a location pin icon in the status bar. Starting with One UI 9, however, the brand adopted the same blue pin the Pixel gets.

For apps that genuinely need location to function well, like weather services or navigation tools, setting permissions to "allow only while using the app" seems reasonable as you'll still get accurate directions or local forecasts. The app won't be able to track your movements once you close it, and that's reassuring.

#313. The reason your laptop can get hot after extended use

Laptops can sometimes run hot, but there are ways to keep them cool.

It's not uncommon to notice your laptop suddenly getting hot, especially after long periods of use. The reason? There are many components inside — such as the processor and battery — packed into a relatively thin chassis, and all that heat has to go somewhere. This heat is often completely normal and caused by things like demanding tasks or background tabs. In these cases, the laptop usually returns to its normal state after a while.

However, if the temperatures get considerably hot and cause your laptop to freeze and restart, there may be something wrong. Luckily, there are ways to figure out the main causes and prevent your laptop from getting too hot.

Every component inside your laptop generates heat, and when you're running more demanding workflows, that heat tends to build up quickly. Playing 3D games or editing videos are just a couple examples of workflows that place greater demands on the hardware. But sometimes, even background processes and startup programs can quietly keep the CPU and memory busy, too. Not only that, but ordinary tasks like installing Windows or macOS updates also increase the load on the hardware, leading to high temperatures and causing the fans to spin faster.

The thing is, these are all completely normal situations. When you close these resource-intensive programs, the laptop tends to cool down within a few minutes. Internal sensors monitor the temperature of the laptop's components to determine when to make the fans spin faster or when to turn them off. Depending on the temperatures, these sensors can also trigger other built-in safeguards to force the laptop to cool down, such as temporarily reducing the speed of the CPU and GPU — a process known as throttling.

Airflow is important for keeping your laptop running at normal temperatures. Most laptop makers don't recommend using them in bed or on a pillow, since the fabric can block the air vents. High external temperatures can also have a major impact on your laptop's heat. Apple, for example, recommends keeping your MacBook between 50 and 95 degrees Fahrenheit, while Microsoft says the Surface's fans will run at a higher speed in environments above 77 degrees Fahrenheit.

Start by checking to see if anything is blocking your laptop's air vents — using it on a flat surface is always the best choice when it comes to preventing overheating. If you're constantly working with resource-intensive software, it might be worth looking into cooling pads, which are trays with fans that sit under your laptop to keep it cooler. If the air vents look dirty, gently clean them with a soft cloth, a brush or compressed air, but never poke anything directly into the vents.

Another way to cool down your laptop is to check for any background processes that are using a lot of CPU power. In Windows, press Ctrl + Shift + Esc to open Task Manager and sort the list by CPU usage to find what's slowing down the processor. There's also a Startup tab that lets you prevent programs from opening automatically when you turn on your laptop. For MacBook users, use Spotlight to open the Activity Monitor app and click the CPU tab. If there are any programs using a lot of CPU resources, try closing them to see if your laptop cools down. Also, there are third-party apps that help you monitor the temperature of your computer's CPU and other components.

If none of this helps, you may need technical support to see if there's something wrong with your laptop. Something might be blocking the fans from working, or the CPU thermal paste might have dried out completely. Another thing to keep in mind is that older laptops tend to overheat more quickly, since they can no longer keep up with the latest software. In that case, your best options are to downgrade to older software or start thinking about getting a new laptop.

#312. How does Google Wallet's notification feed work and can you disable it?

Here's how to check if you've received one of Google Wallet's latest feature rollouts.

If you've looked over the pros and cons of digital wallets and settled on Google's Wallet app, you'll be happy to know an update has dropped for the platform. This update, which began to roll out in October 2026, launched both increased passport recognition and an in-application notification inbox. This update has been added in an effort to have Google Wallet outdo Apple Wallet.

If you've received this new update, the notification feed is easy to find. However, if the thought of more notifications on your phone has you shivering, there are ways to keep this inbox quiet.

If you have Google Wallet's new notification feed feature, there should be a bell icon next to your profile icon in the top-right of the app. If you click on this bell icon, you'll be brought to the notification feed. This page will be totally blank with a bell in the middle if you have no notifications. Otherwise, you'll find your notifications listed here. Remember, the feed is still being rolled out, so you might not be able to find this page at all.

Any notifications you receive about updates to your saved cards and passes will be stored here. This includes changes to traveling information (such as a gate change on a boarding pass), loyalty and gift cards (like point balance updates), incoming offers and more. This notification feed page will simply aggregate these notifications in one place, allowing you to keep track of them from within the app.

One of the reasons you might be happy to see this new notification feed could be rooted in a common problem with Google Wallet, that being the sheer quantity of notifications the app sends. The feed will allow you to find them all in one organized place.

However, depending on your settings, Google Wallet might be taking advantage of Nearby Notifications. Some cards saved to Google Wallet might be sending you offer updates depending on your closeness to a certain location. If you want to avoid notifications from your wallet, this notification feed isn't the thing you want to target. Rather, you want to disable notifications entirely or remove Google's ability to use location data to ping you. You only need to tap on your profile picture, head to Wallet settings > Manage Wallet Notifications and choose the settings you want to tweak. You can also head to Settings > Apps > Google Wallet and either choose Notifications to turn all notifications off or Permissions to deny Google Wallet from using your location.

#309. F1 in Brazil: You’ve got to keep your tires alive

A topsy-turvy grid and intermittent rain resulted in a very entertaining race.

In sports, a “dead rubber” is a match that takes place after the outcome of the series or championship has already been decided. The people in charge hate this*—if you wrap up a title two-thirds of the way through a season, the viewers evaporate, as there’s nothing left at stake. But anyone who decided to skip this year’s Brazilian Grand Prix this past weekend missed one of the year’s most entertaining races, as a combination of a mixed-up starting order, some very fast drivers at the back, and a wet track that dried and then saw rain again resulted in action aplenty.

A peculiarity of F1 is that even if you manage to win a championship, there’s no guarantee you’ll be in a position to defend it. Take McLaren’s Lando Norris, who took the crown in 2025 after a nail-biting fight with his teammate Oscar Piastri and Red Bull’s Max Verstappen. Sadly for Lando, McLaren did not start this year in a position to win races, and even with several upgrades, the team has produced a car that is still very tricky to drive. But this weekend in Brazil, Norris demonstrated the form that earned him the number 1.

Rain on Saturday during qualifying helped jumble the usual running order. It was heavy enough that drivers used the wet weather tire with deep treads, which usually generates so much spray that visibility disappears and running has to be suspended. But with fewer cars on track in the later knockout qualifying sessions, this was not a problem for once. More disruption came from grid penalties for Verstappen and Ferrari’s Lewis Hamilton, both of whom needed to use extra power unit components outside of the season’s allowance.

Norris claimed pole, with this year’s new champion, Kimi Antonelli, alongside in his Mercedes. Row two was filled with the unusual sight of Liam Lawson’s Racing Bull and Nico Hulkenberg’s Audi, followed by Red Bull’s Isack Hadjar and Oliver Bearman’s Haas. Next was George Russell in the other Mercedes, then the even more unusual sight of Fernando Alonso’s Aston Martin, normally found much farther back. One could say the same about William’s Alex Albon, who qualified ninth ahead of the second McLaren of Oscar Piastri.

It looked like it was going wrong for Norris right from the start of the race. Antonelli made a better start and passed him into turn 1. Lawson followed through into second place at the next corner. But Norris kept his head down, and by lap six, he was past the Racing Bull, with about five seconds to make up on Antonelli.

Well-timed pit stops saw Norris switch to the right tires at the right time, and he made none of his rivals’ mistakes, even when the rain returned in the final few laps—at least until the podium ceremony, when he tried to fizz his champagne bottle but instead shattered it.

Keeping one’s tires in the right operating window was vitally important in Brazil, but more than a few struggled with it. The Mercedes duo appeared to struggle to generate sufficient tire temperatures, and their cars looked difficult to handle, prompting plenty of complaints from both Russell and Antonelli to the garage. Russell had been progressing well and ran in the top three early in the race but spun after leaving the pit lane for his first stop, ending up facing the wrong way.

A few laps later, Antonelli spun his own car from the lead, this time going into turn 1, letting Norris back into first. Antonelli then did it again four laps later, ending fourth. Eventually, the Mercedes finished with Russell in fourth and Antonelli in fifth. But the team sealed its Constructors’ Championship, so there was still plenty of celebrating despite the relatively mediocre results (by Mercedes’ high standards).

It’s worth watching the in-car footage from the first couple of laps from both Hamilton and Verstappen, who lined up 20th and 21st out of 22 cars. By the end of lap 1, Hamilton was already in ninth and making things look easy—then again, Brazil was his 400th F1 race start, and he has won more than a quarter of them.

But within four laps, his charge had been blunted. Getting his rear tires up to temperature was key to making up so much ground, but with the track drying, the intermediate tires everyone started the race on had a finite life. With his used up, Hamilton started sliding back down the order. On two occasions, he ended up racing his teammate Charles Leclerc, who started mid-pack with his own penalty.

Unlike in Mexico, Italy, or Holland, the two red cars were able to play nice with each other. But neither managed their tires well, and the best they could do on the road was seventh (Leclerc) and ninth (Hamilton), which became eighth for Leclerc after he was given a five-second penalty for rejoining the track unsafely after running off track while battling Lawson.

Verstappen’s first lap wasn’t quite as good as Hamilton’s—the Red Bull was also never entirely within its setup window all weekend. But taking longer to turn on the rear tires probably contributed to their longevity. While Hamilton’s charge through the pack was blunted, Verstappen was in ninth place by lap 4. By lap 24, he was in second, having taken advantage of Antonelli’s second spin. Verstappen would eventually finish the day in second, 14 seconds off Norris.

F1 takes a weekend off next week, then returns for the night race in Las Vegas.

*Perhaps the prime example of this is NASCAR, which switched to a fairly incomprehensible playoff series it has continued to tweak for years in an effort to prevent anyone from winning the big cup until the last race of the year.

European shares have fallen after sharp losses in Asia and on Wall Street. Doubts over huge AI investments and fears of curbs following the Democrats' US midterm victory have rattled global markets.

#307. Gmail users should know about this little-noticed feature

You can unsend messages on Gmail, but you only have seconds to act.

Whenever you hit Send on Gmail, a small box pops up in the bottom left corner of your screen that will let you undo the action before it's too late. It says Message sent along with the options Undo and View message. If you've suddenly spotted a typo or remembered a piece of information that you forgot to include in your original message, the "unsend" feature could be your best of friend. However, it only shows up for a very short of amount of time, and you have to hit Undo very, very quickly.

To be clear, the feature doesn't literally "unsend" an email that has already been sent. Once it's out there, you can't claw it back from your recipient's inbox, even if it hasn't been opened yet. What it does is delay the service from dispatching your email to give you the chance to stop it from going through. That is why you're only given a short amount of time to decide. Since it only delays your email from being sent, your intended recipient doesn't get notified about it and will not know that you've unsent an email. Take note that hitting View message before the waiting time ends also means you're ending your chance early to unsend your email.

The good news is that you can lengthen the amount of time you have to hit Undo, so you can give your messages a quick final read before Gmail pushes them through. You'll have to manage your expectations, though: Even if you choose the longest amount of time available, it's still pretty short. And no, you still can't take back emails that have already landed in your recipient's inbox.

To choose and extend the amount of time you have to unsend a message, go to Gmail on desktop and click or tap on the Gear icon at the top right corner of the screen. In the Quick settings panel, choose See all settings. In the General settings page, scroll down until you see the Undo Send section, where you'll find the option to change your cancellation period.

It will be set to the feature's default of five seconds, but you can change it to 10, 20 or 30 seconds. That half-a-minute period is the longest you can delay your email's dispatch, but it could be long enough to prevent disaster when sending out especially important messages. Don't forget to hit Save changes at the bottom of the General settings page for the new cancellation period to take effect.

Chelsea has spent over $600 million on new signings. Here's why that's significant

Potter managed to win just 10 of the 28 league matches this season and leaves the team 11th in the Premier League table.

Eventually, after previously sticking by its manager, Blues ownership agreed.

With 10 games left in the league and a Champions League quarterfinal against Real Madrid on the horizon, Chelsea still has plenty to play for.

Assistant coach Bruno Saltor will take over on an interim basis, but Boehly and his team must now look for a long-term replacement capable – and willing – to bring order to Chelsea’s chaos.

Potter’s sacking is yet another sign of just how transient managers can be in the top-flight of English football, especially when results aren’t instantaneous.

There has now been a record 12 sackings in the Premier League so far this season, with Potter leaving his position the same day Leicester boss Brendan Rodgers was sacked from his.

The previous record was 10 manager changes in a single season, set in four other campaigns, most recently in 2017/18.

#303. Nvidia CEO's affair becomes a national security problem

Nvidia is probing whether Chinese intelligence targeted its CEO through an affair.

Nvidia’s chief executive Jensen Huang has admitted having an affair two days after the FT reported his alleged romantic relationship with a woman the US intelligence community believes was working as a spy for China.

Huang revealed the affair in an internal message to employees on Sunday that was obtained by the FT.

“I owe my family and all of you an apology,” Huang said in the message. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

In a separate statement, Nvidia said its board would open an independent investigation “in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort.”

Huang said he would fully cooperate. “My responsibility is to serve you and be worthy of your trust,” he said. “I have fallen short of that responsibility. I am fully committed to Nvidia and will work to earn back your confidence.”

The FT reported on Friday that the US believed Huang had a romantic relationship with a Chinese woman who was recruited to spy for China’s Ministry of State Security intelligence agency.

American officials this year wrote a memo, based on information from US intelligence, which said the Nvidia boss had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based customer of the chipmaker, according to people familiar with the document.

The affair is a damaging blow to the Nvidia chief, who in recent years has become the face of the AI boom, one of the technology’s loudest champions, and transformed the Silicon Valley group into the first $5 trillion company.

It also comes at a critical juncture for the wider AI sector, which faces a deepening political backlash in the US and growing investor concern over whether the hundreds of billions of dollars spent on infrastructure will pay off.

Nvidia’s Frankfurt-listed shares were down more than 6 percent in early trading on Monday.

Huang’s admission of an affair comes after Nvidia previously dismissed suggestions of an improper relationship with Alice Huang, saying the interactions were “nothing unusual or unexpected” when dealing with an Nvidia customer.

The American government memo, which said US intelligence believed the Megaspeed executive had been recruited by the MSS, was drafted just before President Donald Trump traveled to Beijing for a summit with Xi Jinping.

Jensen Huang has developed a close relationship with Trump, which has frustrated US national security officials who believe he is too willing to sell advanced chips to China that could harm American security.

Asked about Huang on Sunday, Trump told reporters he had done an “amazing job” for the US. Asked if he regretted not acting on the intelligence about the affair, Trump said: “No, I don’t think so. I think he’s an honorable guy. I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it.”

“I have not spoken to him about it. I just know he’s a fine person, but you never know what is going on,” Trump added.

After broadly staying out of politics, Huang started to build relations with Trump after the president called him out of the blue on his birthday.

He quickly became one of Trump’s favorite executives, accompanying him on state visits, including to China earlier this year and the UK last year.

Huang has repeatedly lobbied Trump to adopt a more permissive approach to shipping Nvidia’s AI chips to China, after the company was entirely cut off from the critical market by new export controls in 2025.

The resulting deal—with Trump permitting the export of Nvidia’s older-generation H200 chips to China, with the US government taking a cut of sales—had a minimal impact on the Silicon Valley company’s sales, as Beijing pushed back against its technology. But it further angered China hawks in the US administration.

The US government had already been investigating Megaspeed over concerns that it was smuggling Nvidia chips into China.

Nvidia on Sunday said a committee of independent directors, helped by external lawyers, would oversee the investigation.

“The board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders,” the company said. “While the board has no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised, the board’s inquiry will examine all available information.”

Huang, who co-founded Nvidia as a video-game graphics card company in 1993, is one of the longest-serving founder-CEOs in Silicon Valley. More than most technology groups, Nvidia’s success is tethered to that of its CEO.

Huang leads a close and loyal core team of about half a dozen executives, acting as its chief salesman on the international stage while directly managing dozens of direct reports who keep the company running.

He has credited this “flat” organizational structure for the speed at which the company operates, helping it to stay ahead of rivals in the AI chip market and increasing its market valuation from roughly $1 trillion in 2023 to $4.5 trillion today.

Elon Musk, a close ally of Huang, was among the first to offer support, agreeing with a social media post that he was “one of the greatest entrepreneurs of this generation” and “irreplaceable for America’s AI leadership.”

But John Moolenaar, the Republican head of the House China committee who, with other lawmakers, has requested Huang to testify before Congress, said: “It is vital that Jensen Huang address our national security concerns in Congress related to China’s access to sensitive technology and information.”

#302. How to use your iPhone to take the best picture of the Moon

With some easy adjustments, you can upgrade your lunar shots.

Trying to snap a picture of the moon with your iPhone can be an exercise in frustration. You frame it up and everything seems fine, but after clicking the shutter, your photo is either a ball of light with no detail or a blurred mess.

So what happened, and what can you do about it? Even with fancy AI algorithms, cameras can't break the laws of physics when shooting a bright object at night. And unlike some devices, iPhone cameras don't resort to trickery when taking lunar snaps, so the photo quality is entirely up to you and how you set the camera. Here's how to do it right.

Everyone wants their moon photos to be sharp and well exposed, showing all the craters and features you see with your naked eye. The challenge is that the Moon is very bright (it's illuminated by the Sun, after all) and the night sky is extremely dark. Your camera's exposure system always tries to average light, so it often overexposes to "brighten" the surrounding sky. As a result, the moon can often become overexposed and look like a textureless circle.

Your moon photos may also be blurry. This happens because when your iPhone tries to boost exposure, it must lower the shutter speed to 1/30th of a second or even lower. As a result, any hand shaking or other movement can blur the image, even with your iPhone's stabilization system.

Finally, you may find that the moon isn't large enough in your shots. The latest iPhone 17 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro models have up to an 8x "optical-quality" zoom and 40x digital zoom, while the iPhone 15 Pro and iPhone 16 Pro models offer a 5x optical zoom and 25x digital zoom. The iPhone 17 and iPhone 18 standard models have 4x optical and 10x digital zoom. Whatever model you own, zoom in as much as you can so that the moon is clearly visible.

You can get a reasonable moon shot handheld, but your best bet is a tripod. Any cheap one will do, including models like the Amazon Basics 64-inch tripod that includes a smartphone holder. However, I'd recommend spending at least $20 on something higher quality and more adjustable, like the Amazon Basics 50-inch tripod. If you get one like that, you'll also need an inexpensive, tripod-compatible smartphone holder like this one.

If you're having trouble justifying the cost of a tripod, you can also use it for long exposures (to blur waterfalls or create traffic light trails), panoramas, hands-free TikTok-style recordings and video calls. If you really don't want to spend the money, though, you can prop your iPhone against a book or coffee cup, then use the iPhone camera's three-second timer (one of the six-dot menu options) to take the photo. Otherwise, you could jostle your phone when you hit the shutter button and your moon shot will be blurry.

While the recently-launched iPhone 18 Pro's native camera offers manual controls for shutter speed and ISO, older iPhones do not. However, you can adjust two separate exposure settings. The first of those is called exposure and the other is brightness.

To set the first, go to the six-dot menu and tap "Exposure," one of the nine settings on recent iPhones like my 16 Pro Max. Then drag the slider to the right to set it to -2EV, which lets in the least light. This setting will stay through your photo session as long as you don't close the Camera app.

Once your iPhone is set up, tap and hold on the moon for several seconds to lock the autofocus and exposure. Then, slide the "brightness" slider (with a sun icon) that appears to the right down to the desired setting (usually close to the minimum).

Once you do so, you'll start to see the moon's craters and detail appear. Make sure you can see the maximum detail possible, even if the brightness seems too low (you can always increase it after you take the photo). Then press the shutter button, and voila, you have a beautiful, clear shot of the moon. Note that both the exposure and brightness must be adjusted at or near the minimum possible, or your moon photo will lack detail.

If you want to capture video instead of a photo, follow the same process. Change your iPhone's setting to "Video," then zoom in on the moon (15x max for recent Pro cameras) and frame the shot. Make sure Exposure is at -2EV, then tap and hold on the moon to lock focus. Drag the brightness slider down to the desired setting until you see the level of detail you want. Then, hit the record button and capture the desired length of video. To extract a photo, play your video back, stop on the desired frame and take a screenshot.

One quick note on resolution. You may notice that your iPhone moon pictures look pixelated. At 25x digital zoom, the effective resolution on an iPhone 15 Pro or 16 Pro (both with 12MP 5x telephoto cameras) is just 0.48 megapixels. The camera automatically upscales it to 12MP to match your other photos, but the detail is already lost. The iPhone 17 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro models do a bit better with their 48MP sensors and 4x optical zoom, which reduces to an effective 1.24MP resolution at a 25x zoom before upscaling.

If you want more control for moon photos than Apple's Camera app offers, you could upgrade to the iPhone 18 Pro, but the App Store also has other options. Moontake is a free app built for moon photography that uses the longest telephoto lens by default and sets the proper exposure automatically. It also displays astronomical data like the lunar azimuth and altitude. You can also use apps that give you granular control over all types of photography (ISO and shutter speed control), like Halide Mark III and ProCamera.

→ Baca artikel asli

#301. Windows 11 is finally adding an easier way to disable the Sticky Keys pop-up

There’s a checkbox that simply turns it off.

Microsoft is finally making it easier to disable the Sticky Keys pop-up. This window shows up whenever a user hits Shift five times in succession and lets users activate multi-key shortcuts, like Ctrl+Alt+Del, with a single key.

There's just one problem. Sticky Keys are incredibly useful as an accessibility feature, but not everyone needs them. However, the pop-up rears its head each and every time Shift is pressed five times consecutively. This has been a frustration for long-time Windows users who have no interest in Sticky Keys and are annoyed by constant pop-ups. Shift is the default button for many actions in PC games, including sprinting and walking.

We're adding the ability to permanently turn off the prompt to enable sticky keys from the pop up itself pic.twitter.com/kWBYmGPA6M

— Jen Gentleman 🌺 (@JenMsft) November 7, 2026

The solution is downright simple. The pop-up now arrives with a checkbox that says "never turn on Sticky Keys with this shortcut." Clicking the checkbox ensures the annoying window goes away permanently.

There's a spot of bad news here. The checkbox is currently only available to those enrolled in the Windows Insider program. It's just an experimental beta release for now. However, this has been a long-requested feature so it's likely to make the jump to all Windows 11 users in the future.

There's a more traditional way to turn off Sticky Keys, while we wait for general availability. Navigate to "Settings" and then "Accessibility" and finally "Keyboard." Make sure "Sticky Keys" is toggled to the off position. Next, make sure "Keyboard shortcut for Sticky Keys" is also toggled to the off position.

It might be hard to believe, but those wacky Sticky Keys are more than 30 years old. They were first introduced in Windows 95.

#297. SpaceX now fundamentally an "infrastructure company," senior official says

“Grok analyzing the same video is 99.9 percent perfect.”

For a long time, SpaceX has been synonymous with the development of newer, larger, and ever-more-reusable rockets. But that began to change over the last decade with the introduction of Starlink, and the change has accelerated during the last 18 months as the company has pivoted to data and artificial intelligence.

At last week’s UP.Summit conference in Florida, the vice president of launch at SpaceX, Kiko Dontchev, offered some of the most revealing comments to date about the company’s changing focus, how it is embracing AI, and why the Falcon 9 rocket—which has dominated global launch for a decade and remains the world’s preeminent launch vehicle—is firmly on the road to retirement.

“In the last couple years, when I talk about SpaceX, yes, we are a rocket company, but fundamentally we are an infrastructure company at this point,” Dontchev said. “We’re building infrastructure on the ground and low Earth orbit, soon on the Moon, and obviously eventually Mars.”

Earlier this year, SpaceX acquired one of founder Elon Musk’s other companies, xAI. SpaceX engineers were already using xAI’s tools, Dontchev said, but in recent months, employees have really started to figure out how artificial intelligence can speed up their work.

“What’s happened at SpaceX is we’ve really gone through a huge acceleration of adoption,” Dontchev said. “Even just a year ago, we weren’t adopting it that quickly, but really starting this year, we started to adopt it extremely rapidly. And the acceleration we have seen in our ability to do engineering is unlike anything I’ve ever seen at the company in my 16 years.”

Tasks that would take SpaceX employees hours to days now take seconds to minutes, he said. And it’s not just the engineers. Dontchev said the work of identifying foreign object debris, or FOD, in aerospace parts has been largely automated using the AI assistant Grok. This debris can cause catastrophic damage to engines and other spacecraft components.

“We’ve automated being able to detect FOD,” he said. “We’ll have an inspector go in and borescope a tube, and we find that humans are about 94 percent perfect (at) finding a piece of it. Grok analyzing the same video is 99.9 percent perfect.”

SpaceX has also automated visual inspections of other hardware it produces, such as Starlink customer terminals, due to similar gains in accuracy. Dontchev said AI is now being used at every level of the company’s operations.

“We’ve truly started to integrate it to where every single person, including the technicians and the technician leaders, are starting to use it in their day-to-day, and it’s completely leveled the playing field and accelerated people’s growth,” he said.

Since SpaceX’s founding in 2002, a community of spaceflight enthusiasts gravitated to the company because of Musk’s vision for humanity becoming a multiplanetary species, beginning with the settlement of Mars. But concern has grown in this group that SpaceX is drifting away from that vision. Musk has recently made a hard pivot toward the deployment of orbital data centers, envisioning hundreds of thousands of solar-powered satellites providing computing services to users on Earth. Musk has largely stopped talking about sending humans to Mars, except in passing.

Dontchev insisted that human settlement and Mars remain part of the core of SpaceX’s aspirations.

“We have come to understand that this tool is critical to us accelerating our pace of innovation,” he said of artificial intelligence. “Without AI, I think we still would have gotten to the Moon. I think we still get to Mars with SI. We’re going to get there so much faster.”

Dontchev arrived at SpaceX just before the company flew its first Falcon 9 rocket in mid-2010. But because the company was never able to solve second-stage reuse, SpaceX needs to move on, he said.

“Falcon 9, I love that rocket to heart,” he said. “I’ve launched many of them, all of them, in fact. But it’s still not fully reusable. We still throw that second stage away every single flight. Starship—we’re going to catch both the first stage and the second stage.”

For 100 launches a year, it is possible to fly a partially reusable rocket, he said. But for a company that aspires to launch thousands of rockets a year, and eventually maybe even 10,000, the cost and time lost in manufacturing a new second stage for every launch is simply not sustainable.

It is also significant that Dontchev spoke less about Starship’s payload capacity and more about its productivity per launch. The first orbital Starship launch, in late September, carried larger Starlink V3 satellites. And in the aggregate, this single mission carried 10 times the bandwidth of the Starlink payload launched by a Falcon 9.

Flying Starship from Florida, he said, will further improve capacity compared to Texas launches. Launching from Florida offers more favorable trajectories to certain orbits, potentially allowing Starship to carry more satellites without expending propellant on dogleg maneuvers. Dontchev said a single Florida Starship launch will be able to carry up to 20 times the bandwidth of a single Falcon 9.

To that end, the pad infrastructure for Starship’s first launch tower at Kennedy Space Center’s Launch Complex 39A is nearly finished. The company is also developing two additional pads in Florida.

“Pad 39A is effectively complete,” he said. “We’re going to transport boosters and ships over barge here to the Cape. We’re hoping to actually do the first transports this year in December, and then maybe even fly by the end of the year.”

Starship is clearly being optimized to serve SpaceX’s launch needs. But what about commercial launch customers that have relied on Falcon 9 rockets over the last decade?

During his remarks, Dontchev did not address widespread concerns in the US commercial space industry and beyond that SpaceX has not moved to replace the Falcon 9’s lost capacity with Starship. For now, multiple sources have indicated, SpaceX is holding Starship launches in the coming years for its own needs, and potentially those of NASA and other government customers.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#296. Apple starts offering $50 extended usage packs for AI features in Creator Studio

It’ll cost you to access Logic Pro’s AI assistant after you reach the monthly usage limit.

Apple has made it clear for a while that Siri AI is bound by daily usage limits and that it will offer expanded access via iCloud+ plans. The company hasn't shed more light on exactly how that will work, but it has revealed how Creator Studio users can continue wielding the AI features of those apps after they reach the regular limits.

It has started selling $50 intelligence usage packs for Creator Studio, and Logic Pro is the first app to support these. Apple updated Logic Pro last month to include an assistant that's powered by its own models as well as those from third parties. The company said the chatbot can help "creators and producers navigate production workflows, start a new song, set up a vocal track from a single prompt or refine an active project."

As noted by 9to5Mac, folks can view their AI usage in the app by going to Logic Pro on the menu bar, then Intelligence Features > Show Usage. The window that pops up includes a Buy More Usage button — clicking this takes you to the option to buy an intelligence usage pack.

A support page explains that users with active Apple Creator Studio subscriptions in the US, UK, Canada, Brazil, France, Germany, India and Mexico can buy intelligence usage packs to keep using AI features after they reach their monthly usage limit. A pack is tied to the Apple Account used to purchase it — there's no Family Sharing support.

"Unused additional usage purchased with intelligence usage packs roll over from month to month and expire 12 months after purchase," Apple added. "Additional usage gets consumed for relevant features only after you've reached your monthly limit in any given month."

It seems likely that Apple will expand intelligence usage packs to other Creator Studio apps, such as Final Cut Pro and Pixelmator Pro. It's interesting that Apple is opting for this approach over a regular subscription, but perhaps the company has other offerings planned for the future. For what it's worth, Creator Studio apps are available via a subscription or one-time purchase.

#292. 5 Nintendo Switch 2 features everyone should know about

The Switch 2 has lots of little tricks to discover: some inherited from the original Switch and some new.

Chances are that you aren't taking full advantage of everything your Nintendo Switch 2 can do. It's easy to jump right into games and ignore the various settings that help your system run at its best.

The Switch 2 is a pretty direct upgrade to the original Switch — its design, OS, controllers and other aspects are refinements rather than something entirely new. That means many of the Switch 1's tips and tricks work on the newer console too, so if you're a Switch veteran you might already know where to look. But there are other easy-to-miss improvements to find, and they're unique to the newer system.

Whether it's making controls more comfortable or repurposing hardware you already have, you should know about the Switch 2's neat little features. For instance, you might have overlooked the option to share games with friends, which can save you all money. Try these features out before you jump into a game on your next session.

The Joy-Con 2 controllers are larger than the original models but still small enough to sink between the couch cushions or lose sight of underneath a blanket. Next time you can't find your controllers, your console can help. Open the Controllers page from the main menu, then hit Search for Controllers.

All compatible controllers you've paired to the system will appear; to find one, highlight it and hold L or R (or press the touchscreen). While you're holding the button, the controller will vibrate and play a sound, helping you track it down. If you can't find one, try walking around with the console to get back in range of it.

There are a few caveats here. While you can pair original Switch controllers with a Switch 2, this feature can't locate them. Third-party Switch controllers may or may not work. You can't ping a dead controller with this, and it won't find a controller that's paired with another console.

Most laptops, phones and similar devices now include a feature to cap their charging at 80 percent (or similar values). This is designed to protect the health of the device's battery over several years, since keeping lithium-ion batteries at 100 percent for extended periods wears them faster than lower percentages.

Your Switch 2 can also do this: open Settings and scroll to System. About halfway down, you'll see a toggle titled Stop Charging Around 80-90%. Turn this on, and your system will limit itself when it's on the dock or otherwise charging. If you're headed on a long trip, feel free to shut this off first to fully juice up. But in general, especially if your Switch 2 sits on the dock most of the time, this is worth having on.

Speaking of charging, the System Battery (%) option next to this is appreciated, too. It will show the exact battery level instead of a general symbol.

While many modern games have expansive control scheme options, sometimes you can't adjust exactly what you'd like. For those cases, you're able to remap buttons at the system level. Open Settings and go to Accessibility. At the top, choose Button Mapping — if you plan to use this often, enable Button Mapping in Quick Settings and a shortcut to this tool will appear when you hold the Home button.

In this menu, you'll see each connected controller on the left side. Select one and choose Edit Controller's Mappings to show its buttons, where you're free to remap most of the inputs to anything else. For example, you could swap the ZR and ZL buttons with R and L, or move an inconvenient input to a button that's in closer reach.

The C button is of particular interest; if you don't use GameChat, you can disable the button to avoid accidental activation. Alternatively, turn it into a secondary input for another button, like a D-pad direction or pressing one of the sticks. Check the Control Stick Settings if you'd like to swap the left and right sticks or use a Joy-Con 2 vertically when a game intends you to hold it horizontally.

While you can't apply a remap to a certain game, you can save up to five profiles per controller and swap them as needed. After hitting Save, choose Save as a Preset and give it a name. When remapping is applied, you'll see a blue wrench icon at the bottom-left of the menu and anywhere else that controller icon appears. Games are not aware of remapping done this way, so if you've swapped ZR and R, for example, you'll need to press the latter when the game prompts the former.

There's one more related option at the bottom of Settings > Controllers & Accessories: Swap A and B. This simple toggle does what it says and is great if you're used to PlayStation or Xbox controllers that have the Confirm button at the bottom and Cancel on the right.

You have options for letting others enjoy your games, thanks to Nintendo's virtual game cards. Any game loaded onto your system is playable by all other users on the console under their own profile. If you have a Nintendo family group set up, other members can borrow your games on their system for two weeks (during which time you're unable to play them).

Open the Virtual Game Cards page on the Home screen to see your collection. Choose one and hit Lend to Family Group Member, then Select a User to Lend To. To do this, you have to be physically near the other person. You can only loan three games at a time, and a borrower can only have a single game "checked out" at once. Either party can return the game early, and you're free to loan it again if desired after 14 days.

There's also the GameShare feature, where you can stream a copy of compatible games so anyone nearby with a Switch or Switch 2 can join you in multiplayer. You can also do this online using GameChat for friends with a Switch 2. Only a few titles (including Donkey Kong Bananza, Clubhouse Games and Super Mario Odyssey) are supported, but it's worth a look for a low-cost multiplayer.

There are a few ways to reduce slowdowns across your Switch 2 so you don't get bogged down in menus all the time. If you're the only user on your system, it's a waste of time to select your profile every time you open a game or the eShop. To avoid this, go to Settings > Users and enable Skip Selection Screen. This option won't appear if your system has multiple users.

You might also be able to minimize loading times in Switch 1 games. These can see smoother performance when playing off the dock if you visit Settings > System > Nintendo Switch Software Handling > Handheld Mode Boost. By enabling this, your Switch 2 runs original Switch titles as if they were in TV mode, which can improve their graphics or performance. As a trade-off, it will likely use more battery, and there are some control quirks (like the touchscreen not working). There's also an option to add this to Quick Settings for easier toggling.

Finally, there's a much better way to share media you create on your Switch 2. To share your screenshots and video clips much faster, open the Gallery icon on the home screen, hit the Settings cog on the left, and pick your profile. Enable Automatic Uploads and your system will upload up to 100 items, held for 30 days. Then on your phone, install the Nintendo Switch app and sign in with your Nintendo account. By tapping the same Album icon in the app, you're able to access your uploads and share them over chat apps, social media or similar.

#291. Claude Code is accidentally exposing secrets all over GitHub

“Do not ask again” prompt leads to passwords, API keys, and more being shared.

Claude Code is leaking sensitive information on public GitHub instances thanks to a partial oversight in the way its settings files are handled. That’s the finding of AI security researchers at Apiiro, who discovered thousands of secrets, including database passwords, API tokens, and root logins, amid Claude Code settings files embedded in GitHub repositories.

The leak stems from the way Claude Code handles user approval for automated shell commands. When a user chooses the “Yes, and don’t ask again” option to approve running a command, that choice gets stored as part of a rule in the local “.claude/settings.local.json” file on the user’s computer. That rule is sometimes coded as a pattern, but the command itself is often written to that settings file as is, including any sensitive information that may be embedded within it.

This isn’t really a significant security issue if the settings file stays on the user’s local machine (assuming that machine doesn’t become compromised itself, of course). And Anthropic notes in its Claude Code documentation that this particular settings file gets added to the developer’s global .gitignore list, which usually prevents it from being shared publicly.

But that .gitignore setting isn’t reflected at the repo level, meaning the sensitive file can be uploaded accidentally if a repo is cloned to another machine or a folder becomes a repo later, for instance. In many cases, Claude Code itself will commit changes to the settings file without the user’s input, Apiiro writes.

Apiiro’s researchers found over 100,000 cases where Claude Code’s local settings file had been uploaded to a public GitHub repository. Of these, only about 1,100 from 63 different organizations contained secrets such as public API tokens, database passwords, or private keys. Even after these files are discovered and removed from a repository, the secrets can persist in forks of the original project if a GitHub user isn’t careful.

Although this security issue is getting more attention now, it was flagged in a Claude Code bug report as far back as August 2025. Other bug reports also noted that the sensitive settings file was sometimes not added to the user’s .gitignore list or that Claude Code could add the sensitive commands to another file that was not protected from git sharing. These bug reports were all closed without action after 60 days of inactivity.

In a comment thread on Hacker News over the weekend, Claude Code’s Boris Cherny said the team is “looking into what we can do to prevent this.” That said, he noted that these settings files should pretty much never be checked in to a user’s git source control and that cases like these seemed to come up only when “the files were manually created and checked in.”

In the meantime, Cherny recommended using Claude Code’s auto mode, which uses classifiers to automatically route “routine permission prompts” without requiring what Cherny calls “manually maintaining finicky allow/deny lists.” But when another user noted that sensitive commands can still be saved to settings files in auto mode, Cherny said that was a “good catch” and added that the team is “thinking through what we can do to improve this.”

Cherny also recommends Claude Code users make use of secret scanners—which can trawl files for sensitive information and scrub it out—and said that the team was considering building this kind of secret scanning into Claude Code itself in the future. That said, the Apiiro researchers found that 215 of the roughly 1,100 public secrets it found in Claude Code settings files were not flagged by two popular secret scanners, gitleaks and TruffleHog.

This is far from the only case of an AI agent unintentionally leaking sensitive information. Last month, Meta suspended the sharing feature on its Muse agent after security researchers found the URLs for those links could be easily guessed.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#286. Pentagon mulls placing fleet of military satellites around the Moon

“Please jump on my cislunar bandwagon because I need some more friends here.”

The US military has published a Request for Information on a federal procurement website signaling interest in deploying a “proliferated cislunar constellation” consisting of an unspecified number of satellites performing an undisclosed mission in the vicinity of the Moon.

The announcement follows statements in recent months from the Space Force’s acquisitions chief for advanced programs, Maj. Gen. Stephen Purdy, that the military was getting serious about extending its reach to the Moon. The Space Force’s most distant satellites today are in geostationary orbit, some 22,000 miles (36,000 kilometers) over the equator, collecting routine surveillance and missile warning data.

Most recently, Purdy spoke of a shift in Pentagon leadership’s thinking about the Moon following an executive order signed by President Donald Trump last December. The order set out the Trump administration’s space policy. One section of the order deals with the US government’s ability to detect, characterize, and counter threats to US space interests from low-Earth orbit to cislunar space, the term of art for the region of space around the Moon.

“I’ve got the Secretary of War and the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs full on stating in public: ‘This is a warfighting domain,’” Purdy said at Space Systems Command’s Industry Days event in October, according to a report in Air & Space Forces Magazine. “And so, I would ask you all, please jump on my cislunar bandwagon because I need some more friends here, along with our deep, passionate crew that have been working on this for years.”

Officials have emphasized the military’s role at the Moon is to protect US interests there as the United States and China ramp up their lunar programs, with a series of robotic missions culminating in crewed lunar landings in the next few years. Some US officials foresee a competition for real estate and resources at the Moon, where there are a finite number of safe landing zones that can access the Moon’s water ice at the south pole.

“We have a role to play, but we’re not the lead,” said Gen. Chance Saltzman, who was the top general in the Space Force until his retirement in August. “I just don’t want to get caught flat-footed when we start to have to protect US interests out there, so we’re along for the ride.”

Now, the Pentagon has taken a preliminary step toward a “Cislunar Space System,” a constellation of military satellites the military says it is considering deploying around the Moon. The new RFI was released by the Department of the Air Force and dated November 2. It asks companies to inform the military on their ability to provide “rapid” and “low-risk” solutions to support the government’s “technical and programmatic objectives.”

The document provided few details about what the Pentagon wants to do around the Moon with the Cislunar Space System, other than it will consist of up to five spacecraft, each with a government-provided payload, to meet military “mission needs” in late 2029.

The RFI hints at a vision for a much larger fleet of satellites in cislunar space. The first five satellites “may serve as seed vehicles that will inform the acquisition strategy for a potential future proliferated cislunar constellation,” the RFI said.

“The government intends to award up to five firm fixed price preliminary design study contracts upon government consideration of the market information collected with this RFI and all other sources of information relevant to the effort,” military officials wrote on the government procurement website.

Responses are due by November 24. It is unclear when the Pentagon might move forward with firm contracts, and the language in the RFI does not commit the military to doing so. The standard commercial satellite development cycle can run two or three years, so it’s a safe bet that military officials would want deals signed sometime in 2027 to meet their 2029 schedule goal.

The secretary of the Air Force announced in September that the US military has placed unspecified weapons in orbit. But don’t expect the Space Force to send weapons to the Moon any time soon.

While NASA focuses its lunar program on human landings, a Moon Base, resource exploration, and scientific research, military satellites at the Moon could perform similar roles as they do in Earth orbit. These functions could include tracking and characterization of other objects in cislunar space, navigation and communications, and surveillance.

The Defense Department has already had a presence at the Moon. A joint NASA-DOD spacecraft named Clementine discovered the first hints of water ice inside permanently shadowed craters at the lunar south pole.

A military-led mission named Oracle-Mobility is scheduled to launch in mid-2027 on a trajectory toward the Moon. The $75 million Oracle-M mission, formerly known as the Defense Deep Space Sentinel, is led by the Air Force Research Laboratory, which says the satellite will be “highly mobile and will transit throughout cislunar space and experiment with techniques to maintain custody of objects in this region.”

“This mission will allow AFRL to assess strengths and weaknesses of navigation techniques needed for future operational systems,” AFRL says on its website.

A second Oracle mission, Oracle-Prime, is supposed to be ready for launch by the end of 2027. Its mission will involve detecting and tracking other satellites and debris from a position near Earth-Moon Lagrange Point 1, a gravitationally stable location about three-quarters of the way to the Moon.

Each of the Oracle missions will only involve a single spacecraft, but the recent RFI from the Department of the Air Force suggests a move toward a more sustained military presence around the Moon.
